Now, regarding cobalamin binding: Cobalamin (vitamin B12) contains a cobalt ion in the center. In cobalamin-dependent enzymes and binding proteins, the cobalt is typically coordinated axially. The lower axial ligand is often a histidine residue (from the protein), and the upper axial ligand varies (methyl, adenosyl, aquo, etc.).

The classic example is methylmalonyl-CoA mutase and methionine synthase, where a histidine residue serves as the lower axial ligand to the cobalt of cobalamin.

This protein sequence appears to be from a cobalamin-dependent enzyme, likely methionine synthase or a related protein based on the sequence features.

Given the options:
- Gln156 - glutamine could potentially coordinate but is not typical for cobalamin axial binding
- Asp197 - aspartate could potentially coordinate but is not typical
- His193 - histidine is the classic axial ligand for cobalamin cobalt binding
- Asn245 - asparagine could potentially coordinate but is not typical

The answer should be C. His193, as histidine is the well-established axial cobalt-binding residue in cobalamin-binding proteins.
